Washington, D. C.
Attention: Mrs. Miller
RE: ENTRY TO JAPAN FOR MRS. ANGELICA KLEBANOFF
Gentlemen:
This will confirm our telephone conversation of this morning in which the
undersigned requested information regarding the entry to Japan for his wife.
I again take this opportunity of informing you that I am an American citizen
and am the Secretary-Treasurer of the above American corporation, incorporated
under the State Laws of New York. I first entered Japan in March, 1948, on a
Traders Quota under Military Permit #8244 dated March 1, 1948, and resided ther
until December 1, 1948. During that period, I established semi-permanent
residence as a commercial representative and now hold a Re-entry Permit #982
which was issued to me by the Diplomatic Section of SCAP, prior to my departure
from Tokyo, on November 29, 1948, and which is valid until May 29, 1949.
It is now my intention to return to Japan with my wife where we intend to live
C
for at least two years ao that I may take charge of our Japan office. Furthermore,
S/A
I will definitely not require that SCAP furnish me any logistics for neither
myself nor my wife since I have a house available in Tokyo to take care of my
requirements.
The following information is hereby submitted regarding my wife:
894.111/4-1849

Should you require any further information, please do not hesitate to call or

In the event that the permit will be granted by the 2lst of April, I would be

that be impossible, then I would appreciate the permit being mailed at your
earliest convenience registered air mail to my wife's address as above.
Because it is our intention to remain in Japan for at least two years and since
we are newly married, I would appreciate your kind efforts in expediting this
permit as much as possible so as to enable my wife to spend some time in purchasing
various household and personal effects.
I am enclosing herewith a letter from Oriental Exporters Inc. and a letter from
our bankers, the Irving Trust Company, to substantiate some of the above facts.

RE: ENTRY TO JAPAN FOR MRS ANGELICA KLEBANOFF
Gentlemen:
This is to inform you that Mr. Michael Klebanoff, Secretary-
Treasurer of our firm, is intending shortly to depart for Japan
where he is to take charge of our Japan office.
In view of the fact that he is newly married and intends to
remain in Japan for several years, we would appreciate your
efforts in arranging for the permii for his wife who is to
accompany him to Japan.
Thanking you in anticipation, we are,
Very truly yours,
ORIENTAL EXPORTERS, INC.

April 18, 1949
Division of Protective Services
Department of State
515 22nd Street, N. W.
Washington, D. C.
Attention: Mrs. Miller
Gentlemen:
This letter is written to you at the request of Mr. Michael Klebanoff,
who we understanu is requesting permission to travel with his wife
in Japan and to remain several years.
Mr. Klebanoff is well known to us as Secretary end Treasurer, also as
a stockholder of Oriental Exporters, Inc., 350 Fifth Avenue, New York,
which company he will represent in Japan. The company has been a
valued depositor of ours since March 1946 and Mr. Klebanoff also has
carried a personal account with us since that year. Balances in the
company's account for the year 1948 averged in excess of $150,000,
and we heve financed the company for substantial amounts on Letters
of Credit and other types of accommodation. Oriental is engaged in
the import and export business and has transacted a considerable volume
with Japan.
It also is our understanding Mr. Klebanoff is a United States citizen
and served in the army from which he received an honorable discharge.
Inasmuch as he is newly married and wishes to depart for Japan at an
early date, we shall appreciate your expediting approval of the neces-
sary permits. In our opinion he is deserving of your confidence and
consideration.
Very truly yours
w. s. Pen
Vice President
I hereby certify that the above
is the signature of Mr. W. E. Petersen,
Vice President of the Irving Trust Company.
Peter S Spinel

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.97)
UNCLASSIFIED
ACTION
No. 74
THE FOREIGN SERVICE
is assigned to
OF THE
UNITED STATES OF AMERICA
Yokohama Branch,
Office of the U. S. Political Adviser,
Yokohama, Japun, April 20, 1949.
2
Subject: Shore Leave for lassengers on Vessels passing
in Transit through Japan.
The consul Jeneral has the honor to transmit the
following information to assist in replying to inquiries
concerning shore leave lor transit passengers on vessels
in Japanese ports.
In general, because of the shortage or absence of
894
conmercial transportation, facilities for billeting or
meals and other considerations for such personnel, un-
restricted shore leave is not granted transit passengers
on vessels callin; at Japanese ports. However, arrange-
ments may readily be made for shore leave for any such
passenger in he event responsible persons attached to,
or servinr with, the Occupation Forces, including members
of foreign diplomatic missions, or local foreign residents,
such as businessmen or missionaries, will agree to be res-
ponsible for the passenger, i.e., principally assure that
he will obtain necessary transportation, billeting, meals,
0
abide by local regulations, and be returned to the vessel
4.
P1
prior to its sailing. Such passengers may also readily
6
广人
exchange United States currency for Japanese currency to
make local purchases. No prior arrangements are necessary
to obtain such shore leave, nor is validation of passports
for travel to Japan required. It would, of course, ob-
viously be desirable that any person desiring to make such
arrangements communicate with the responsible party in
FE
Japan prior to arrival. However, the appropriate Military
11
Government officers have assured this office that military
OFD
personnel concerned with the enforcement of regulations
concerning entry into Japan have been instructed to pro-
vide all reasonable assistance in aiding passengers in
communicating with prospective guarantors and making
arrangements for shore leave; it being the desire to grant
to responsible persons the maximum number of shore leaves
possible within existing policies.
It should also be noted that passengers on the
regular passenger vessels of the American President Lines
may purchase on board the vessels tickets for short sight-
seeing excursions without prior arrangements or validation
of passports. Such excursions are of two types -- one in-
volves a bus trip of either the Tokyo or Kamakura areas
while the vessel is in the port of Yokohama, or if the
vessel is calling at both Yokohama and Kobe, a combined
bus and rail excursion of Kobe, Osaka, Kyoto, Tokyo and
okohama.
As stated
MAY 10 1949
UNCLASSIFIED

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.98)
No. 74
Yokohama, Japan
April 20, 1949
-2-
as stated in our despatch No. 108 or June 17, 1948,
this orfice and that at Kobe are pleased to make arrange-
ments for Foreign Sorvice personnel on ships calling at
either port, and not desiring to take one of the fore-
goin; excursions, to obtain shore leave to visit friends
or other such purposes within the limits of the small
amount of transportation available at each office. While
prior arrangements are not necessary, it would, of course,
be helpful to have advance notice of the arrival of such
personnel.
It should be noted that Japanese currency for use
within Japan may be legally acquired only after arrival
in Japan from authorized banks, Army finance offices,
their agents aboard vessels in port, or other such
facilities at the authorized conversion rate which at
present is ¥270.00 to US $1.00. It is understood that
Japanese currency is being offered at much more favorable
rates by various persons, particularly in Hong Kong and
xA
Shanghai. Apart from the illegality of bringing such
currency into Japan, it should be noted that the greater
part undoubtedly represents the proceeds of transactions
in merchandise smuggled into Japan in violation of the
existing directives of the Supreme Commander, and to the
detriment of American financial and commercial policy
toward Japan. Therefore, this office strongly urges
that Foreign Service personnel, in particular, be in-
formed that they should not purchase or attempt to bring
into this co'ntry Japanese currency so acquired in order
to avoid possible embarassment to themselves as well as
to Foreign Service posts here.

Allied Powers at Tokyo to the various foreign representatives at

"3. In the cases where any country is willing to

=== Page 57 === (confidence 0.99)
2-
of the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powere that the
necessary travel document in lieu of passport, i.e.,
certificate of identity or similar document, be issued
by the country of destination through its diplomatic
mission in Japan or the diplomatic mission of its pro-
tecting power. In cases where this procedure may not
be acceptable to the country of destination for technical
reasons, clearance will not be granted for the Japanese
national concerned to depart Japan."
If Mr. Yazawa is in fact a Japanese national, it is suggested
that the Venezuelan Government may wish to forward to the Department
for transmission to the Department's representative at Yokohama a
travel document (i.e., a certificate of identity or similar document)
for issuance to him, together with the necessary authorization to
visa the document for his entry into Venezuela. It will of course
be necessary that arrangements be made in advance to defray the
transportation costs in connection with Mr. Yazawa's travel to
Venesuela. The Department should be informed of the arrangements
made in order that the necessary assurances may be given to the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers.

and Japan.
220874.1
I married a Japancse girl. I have a paby five months old
April 11.

take my wife and baby back to Japan to work in the Occupation of

us veterans pernission to marry. Me and my wife got back to the

us veterans love you both very much.
Dear r. President, my wife is getting thin and she crys all

sister. ir Fcrces said that I could not take her back, but Mr. President
if I get permission from you I can work in Japan because I have my
Form 57 into nir Forces. I am a mechanic and also a heavy equipment
operator.

her ana tne baby.

ESSENTIAL RECUIREMENTS WHEN MAKING APPLICATION FOR PASSPORT
1. An application for a passport must be executed before a clerk of a Federal court ora State court a-
thorized by law to naturalize aliens, or before an agent of the Department of State. An application executed
before any other official will not be accepted. Passport Agents of the Department of State are located in New
York (Subtreasury Bldg., also at Hockefeller Center, International Bldg.) and San Prancisco (126 Federal Office
Bldg.). In Washington it is desired that applications be executed in the Passport Division of the State De-
partment.
NECESSARY PROOF TO ESTABLISH AMERICAN CITIZENSHIP
 '? Al do ument, uh as birth certificates, baptismal (ertificates, certified copies of records,
f livit. tuhnitt1 a4 vi dence of I he Ameri(an  il izenship of an applicant for A passport must give

that the birth or baptism was recorded short ly after birth
2. A native American citizen must submit with his application for a passport a birth certificate, or, if
such acertificate is not obtainable, a baptismal certificate or acertified copy of the record of baptism. If
neither of these cerrificates is obtainable, the applicant should submit in lieu thereof an affidavit executed
by a parent, brot her, sister, or other relative, preferably an older person, or the physician who attended the
birth, setting forth the date and place of birth of the applicant. lf an affidavit of a relative or physician
(annot be obtained, an affidavit of some ot her reputable person having knowledge of facts which enable'bim to
test ify as to the place and date of birth should be submitted. In the affidavit a brief statement should be
ma le -howing ho and through what source knowledge of the place and date of birth was acquired.
3. À ;erson wh) (lains American citizenship through birth abroad of a native or naturalized American
fither of mother should prove the parent's birth in the lnited States or naturalization as a citizen of this
ountry ty dx unentary rvidence of the kind indicated in paragraph 2 or 6.
4. ta) A wonan who was married to an American cit izen prior to September 22, 1922, must submit evidence
of her husband's Anerican citizenship.
(b) An Americ in woan who was marrled to an American (itizen or to an alien on or after September 22,
1922, rust subn1t evi1dence of her on c1tizenship.
) An Anvrican wnman who lost American citizenship bymarriage toanalien, but whoalleges that after
the ter inat ion of the rurital re lat 1on and prior to September 22, 1922, she resumed American citizenship, must
submit ihe evidence that she was an American (it izen at the t ime of marriage. If the marital relation is alleged
to have bern terminated bydivorce, a certified copy of the decree of court granting the absolute divorce should
be suturitted. A legal separation or an interlocutory decree of divorce does not terminate marriage.
(1) An American woman who lost citizenship by marriage to an alien prior to September 22, 1922, and
resuned American citizrnshig after that date must submit documentary evidence of her naturalization.
5. A person who claims American citizenship by naturalization must submit with his application a certifi-
cat f naturalizatlon.
6. (a) A person who claims citizenship through the naturalization of a parent may submit with his appli-
(ation the naturalization certificate of the parent through whom he derived citizenship, or a certificate of
derivative citizenship issued by the cormissioner of limigration and Naturalization under the provisions of
the naturalization laws.
(b) A woman who claims (itizenship through the naturalization of her husband prior to September 22,
ly22, may submit the latter's cert:ficate of naturalization or a derivative certificate of citizenship issued
by the Comissloner of Immigration and Naturalization under the provisions of the naturalization laws.
7. An applicant who holds an expired or unexpired passport issued since January 2, 1918, should submit
the old passport for cancellation. Such document will be accepted as evidence of citizenship if proper docu-
mentary evidence of American nationality was submitted with the previous application. If a previous passport,
issued since January 2. 1918, which has definitely expired cannot be presented for surrender or cancellation,
it is necessary to state briefly in the new application the disposition of the previous passport. However, if
the prevlous passport has not definitely expired it is necessary to submit under oath a separate statement set-
ting forth in circunstantial detail the disposition of the valid passport.
PHOTOGRAPHS
8. Two recent ly taken photographs (duplicate) must be submitted of each person named in the application,
one affixed tothe application, the other, signed by the applicant, nust accompany the application unattached.
A group photograph should be used when awife, or wife and children, are inc luded in one application. Photo-
graphs must be full face, on thin paper, with a light background, and not over 3 by 3 inches nor less than
2: by 23 inches in size. Snapshot, newspaper, magazine, or ful1-length photographs will not be accepted.
Photographs printed on photographic paper the back of which is glazed will not adhere to passports and there-
fore will not be accepted.
IDENTIFYING WITNESS
9. The identifying witness must appear in person with the applicant and fill in and sign the affidavit
(on the application form) before aclerk of court or an agent of the Department of State. The identifying wit-
ness should be an adult American citizen (man or woman) who is able to state under oath that he has known the
applicant for at least 2 years; that the applicant is the person he represents himself to be; and that the
facts stated in the application are true tothe best of thewitness' knowledge and belief. An expired American
passport bearing asigned photograph may be used as ident ification in lieu of an identifying witness. In cases
of persons who have not previous ly obtained passports, the applicant or the witness must be known to the clerk
of court orthe Passport Agent, or theapplicant or the witness must be able to establish his identity, beyond
reasonable doubt, by documentary evidence which should be listed on the application. If the applicant or the
witness is not known to the clerk of court or Passport Agent and conclusive documentary evidence of the iden-
tity of either cannot be presented, the applicant will be sequired to obtain as a witness to his applicatiom an
M-27
6-17-48

=== Page 89 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Anerican cit izen establis hed in a recognized profession or bus iness and having his office or place of busiuess
within the jurisd ict ion of the court or passport agency. However, if a c lerk of court or a Pasaport Agent com-
s iders that an applicant has not been sat isfactorily identified, he mmy forward the application to the Depart-
ment of State with a statement of the facts in the case, giving the names of two or more persons with whon the
Department may communicate in order to sat isfy itself of the identity and citizenship of the applicant. A
husband or wife is not acceptab le as an identifying witness of the s pouse unless personally known to the clerk
of court or Pass port Agent.

of the firm, or in his absence fron the person in (harge, showing the countries to be visited and the necessity
for travel therein.
when a missionary applies for a pass port he should submit to the De partment a letter showing the name
of the organization which he represents, the station to which he wishes to proceed, whether he has previously
served abroad as anisslonary, whether he is returning to a station previous ly occupied, whether he will be re-
placing a missionary who is returning to the United States and, if so, the name of the missionary, or whether
he will be an addit ion to t he ex ist ing miss lonary staff. The letter should a lsoshow the date or the approx imate
date of the proposed departure and the travel arrangements which have been made.
FEES FOR PASSPORT AND APPLICATION
11. The total of the fees for passport and application is 1o (89 must accompany the application to
washington and should be tathe form of a postal money order made payable to the Secretary of State, Washington,
D. (., and $1 paid to t he c lerk of court or agent before whom the applicat ion is executed). No fee is collected
for the iss uance or renewal of a pass port to a person pr oceeding abroad on off ic ial bus iness or to members of
his household who des ire toaccompany or join him but the fee of S1 for the execut ion of the application for a
passport must be collected.
12. 'nder the Act of ay 16, 1932, a pass port 1s va lid for 2 years from t he date of issue unless limited
to a shorter period. It may be renewed for a period of 2 years upon payment of fee of $5, but the final date
of expirat ion shall not be more than 4 years from the original date of issue. A passpori which was issued
within the period of 4 years pr ior to the time when applicat ion for renewa! is made may be renewed.
Requests for renewal mmy be made by personal applicat ion or letter addressed to the Department of
State, a Passport Agent, a Consular Officer of the United States, or the Chief Execut ives of Hawaii, Puerto
Rico, the Virgin Is lands, Guam, or American Samoa, and the fee, in currency or postal money order, should ac-
(ompany each request. Drafts or chec ks will not be accepted.

PASSPORT DIVISION
DEPARTMENI OF STATE
Dear Mrs. Shipley:
Somewhat at a loss to know to whom to write regarding this case,
I am taking the liberty of addressing this letter to you. Please be good
enough to see that the right person receives the enclosed copies. One of
my purposes in writing to you is to assure myself that your Division is cog-
nizant of all the factors in this somewhat peculiar situation.
The Rev. Toru Matsumoto, Larchmont Gables, Larchmont, New York, is
a Japanese subject who has been granted permanent residence privileges for
himself and his family in the United States, as from the 16 May 1947.
By action of the Board of Foreign Missions of the Reformed Church
in America, 156 Fifth Avenue, New York 10, New York, on 12 May, Mr. Matsumoto
received appointment as a missionary of that organization, under contract for
5
three years of service on the faculty of their related college in Tokyo, Meiji
Gakuin. He is desirous of starting for his new position soon after the 1lth
of August l949. He intends to go alone, leaving Mrs. Matsumoto, who is a
Japanese subject and his three America-born children in this country.
Mr. Matsumoto's attorney has arranged with the Immigration Service for the
issuance of a "Travel Document" in lieu of a passport. The reason for this
procedure lies in the fact that Mr. Matsumoto is the holder of Passport #371239
issued by the Foreign Minister of Japan on the 31 July 1940--a document which,
it goes without saying, is now invalid. Mr. Matsumoto's attorney is also ar-
ranging to secure for his client proper exit and re-entry permits to be issued
shortly before Mr. Matsumoto takes ship for Japan.
The above gives the main features of the situation as covering Mr.
Matsumoto's plans. My supposition is that he will need, further, to secure
issuance of a Military Permit, just as in the case of other missionary appoint-
ees. The Board of Foreign Missions of the Reformed Church in America guaran-
tees round trip travel from New York and full support for Mr. Matsumoto during
his projected term of service with them. Adequate housing has been set aside
for Mr. Matsumoto by the College in Tokyo. We can therefore state positively
that Mr. Matsumoto will not become a burden on the Japanese people or on the
S
Occupation Forces and will not engage in commercial or other unauthorized ac-
tivities.
mOE
JUN 101949
176031
4

Mrs. Shipley, 5/9/49, 2
On the basis of the above factual statements, the Foreign Missions
Conference would request the issuance of a Military Permit for Mr. Matsumoto.
Thanking you for your always generous cooperation, I am
Yours very truly,
Royal  Fisher, Assistant Secretary
Far Eastern Joint Office, F. M. C.

A-386, May 18, 1949
Reference your A-424 of May 9, 1949 regarding the Sisters of
the Eucharistic Missionaries of the Holy Trinity, all Mexican nationals,
who desire to enter Japan for the purpose of establishing a Catholic
mission there.
In view of the fact that the Swedish Diplomatic Mission at
Tokyo represents Mexican interests in Japan it should present the
applications of these nuns for permission to enter Japan to the Supreme
Commander. Therefore, in accordance with established procedure they
should be advised to request the Mexican Foreign Office to transmit
their applications to the Swedish Mission at Tokyo.
In the event the Swedish Mission at Tokyo is reluctant to act in
this matter it is suggested that the Eucharistic Missionaries of the
111/5-949
Holy Trinity communicate with Father Bruno Bitter, Vice President of
Jochi University, Tokyo, giving the names of the nuns who desire to
enter Japan. In the past Father Bitter has frequently acted as
liaison with SCAP in matters of this kind.

RE: ENTRY TO JAPAN FOR MRS. ANGELICA KLEBANOFF
Gentlemen:
I wish to take this opportunity of first thanking you for your prompt
attention to my request for my wife's permit to Japan which she
received in Havana.
My wife is now with me in New York and is preparing for our trip to
Japan. As you know, both of our permits expire at the end of May, and
we now discover that the first suitable steamer sails from Los Angeles
on the 17th of June, and I, therefore, request that you extend her
5-1049
permit until at least July lst, which is the scheduled date for the
arrival of the SS "President Cleveland" on which we hold reservations
or to be on the safe side, a few days beyond that.
I would appreciate it very much if you could arrange this for us in
view of the fact that my wife is not too anxious to fly which is what
we would have  do in order to enter Japan before the 29th of this
month.
I, in turn, am applying for the extension of my permit through the
Department of Commerce.
Should you be able to arrange for this extension, I would appreciate
your notifying me at the above address.

Very truly yours,

MEMORANDUM
The Chilean Ministry of Foreign Affairs has advised
the Embassy in Washington that the Chilean Government is very much
interested in sending to Japan a person qualified to study the
possibilities of reestablishing in that country a market for Chilean
Nitrate of Soda.
In this connection the Corporación de Ventas de Salitre
y Yodo ( Chilean Nitrate ana Iodine Sales Corporation) has agreed
89
to send to Japan one of its high officials, Mr. James T. Fyfe.
This Embassy is requesting the Department of State to
be good enough to advise the American Embassy in Santiago to grant
to Mr. Fyfe the necessary visa for the purpose indicated above.
9
Mr. Fyfe is a Chilean citizen and has worked for many
years witn the Corporación de Ventas de Salitre y Yodo. He possesss
9
an official Chilean passport.

=== Page 219 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
e/k
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: June 8, 1949.
70
SUBJECT:
Chilean Emtassy's memorandum of July 7 requesting
permission for Mr. Janes 7I. Fyfe to enter Japan.
FW
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Mario Illanes, Commercial Counselor, Chilean Embassy.
894.11
Mrs. Mellor - DS.
COPIES TO:
1/6-749
epo
1—1408
Mrs. Mellor (DS) called Mr. Illanes of the Chilean Embassy
upon receipt o1 the Embassy's memorandum of June 7, 1949 requesting
permission to enter Japan for Mr. James T. Fyfe, who is stated to be
a high official of the Chilean Nitrate and Iodine Sales Corporation,
and inquired if the Embassy could supply certain information in
connection with Mr. Fyfe's application.
Mr. Illanes was somewhat indignant that the "Department" made it
CS/ A
so difficult for a man of Mr. Fyfe's stanuing to enter Japan. Mrs. Mellor
explained to Mr. Illanes that the Department of State did not grant
permission to enter Japan; that such permission was granted only by the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers at Tokyo; and that the Department
was merely the agency which transmitted SCAP's decision. Mr. Illanes
then further elaborated upon Mr. Fyfe's connection with the Government
and stated a Japanese mission had been in Chile two months ago in
connection with the matter upon which Mr. Fyfe desires to go to Japan.
Mrs. Wellor stated that she would submit the application to the
Pacific Military Permit Branch, Department of the Army, for transmission
to SCAP and she asked Mr. Illanes that if the information proved to be
insufficient if the Embassy would send a note to the Department
incorporating the supplemental information which he had given to her
over the telephone. He said he would be glad to do so.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: June 17, 1949.
SUBJECT:
Extension of validity uate to enter Japan for Hans A. Ries.
FW 894.
PARTICIPANTS:
Manager, Philipp Brothers, Inc.,
70 Pine Street,
New York 5, N. Y.
Telephone Bowling Green 9-1313
Mrs. Mellor - DS
111/6-1649
COPIES TO:
1-1493
Mrs. Mellor called Philipp Brothers to inform the Company that
its representative, Mr. Hans A. Ries, had been granted an extension
of his validity date to enter Japan to August 21, 1949. Mrs. Mellor
wanted to know if Mr. Ries was in the country or abroad. The manager
said Mr. Ries would be in Karachi within a few days and could be reached
at the Pan-american air Lines. Mrs. Mellor informed him that the
Department could not send a telegram there in view of the fact that it
would have to be sent to an Embassy or a Consulate since a certified
copy of the telegram would have to be used by Mr. Ries in lieu of a
CS/A
military permit document to permit him to enter Japan. The manager
then said Mr. Ries would be in Hong Kong en route to Japan and requested
the Department to send the telegram to the American Consulate General
there and Philipp Brothers would communicate with Mr. Ries and advise
him to call at the Consulate General.
173792
myE

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
True Copy
Hans Friedel
33, Gartenstrasse, Ludwigsburg
Germany - American Zone
June 10, 1949
sir,
Kindly permit me to approach you in the following connection and I would be
muoh obliged to you for giving me advice and assistance if possible.
I am a German national, born of German parents in Germany on August 2, 190l.
Since 1922 I have been living in foreign countries (Europe, Asia Minor, South and
Central America). I arrived in Japan in March 193?, coming from China, upon my own
accord and never left that country until I was forcibly repatriated together with my
family (wife and child) in Febrary 1947. I am a baker by trad and in Japan I was
teaching Japanese bakers foreign-style baking etc. introducing foreign-made
ingredients (Fleishman yeast). I married a Japanese national in 1935 according to
Japanese rites which marriage, contrary to former procedure, the German consul at
Yokoham refused to acknowledge due to the then existing racial laws of the Nazis.
I was never a member of the Nazi party nor of one of its affiliated organizations.
In 1945 I was arrested and imprisoned on account of anti-Nazi activities. End
ofAugust 1945, that is &fter 3 months I was set free by the American Army.
End of January 1947 I was informed by armed officers of the US Army that I would
be repatriated to Germany together with my family in abt. 2 weeks time. We were
allowed to take our personel belongings with us, everything else including
jewelry was taken away.
We were brought aboard the American ship "Marine Jumper" off Nagahama middle of
February 1947 and it was only days later that we were handed written repatriation
orders (Special Orders no. 32 of 7 Feb 47 by Hqs Eighth Army, AP0 343 - Auth:
Memo for Imperial Japanese Government, 13 Jan 47, AG O14.33 (13 Jan 47) GA
(SCAPIN 1462) subject: Repatriation of German and Austrian Nationals.")
The Allied Powers had declared that they were fighting the last war to liberate
the German people and the world from the yoke of national-socialism and
militarism. It is especially the American Government which so loudly proclaims
upholding the fundamental human rights and here we are shipped like cattle without
the American authorities ever going to the trouble of offering us a chance to defend
our human rights. I dare claim that the US Government has to live up to its committ-
mentseven when nationals of former enemy countries are concerned and since this has
not been done our forced repatriation is nothing but an outrage carried on under the
cloak of secrecy and false pretences.
Due to the fact that according to reports in the US armed forces' newspapers
both in Japan and Germany "Ex-Nazis were repatriated" and due to the fact German
nationals are still residing in Japan I claim that I hage not been treated in
accordance with my status.
It may concern you that the forced repatriation comprised abt. 12o0 German
nationals, all party members, with the exception of a few other Gerans whose
wives are also of Japanese parentage and who had likewise no connection with
the Nazi party. Our wives are suffering terribly from this totally umwarranted
repatriation due to entirely different conditions in climte, food, and life in
general. We, therefore, justly claim that we be shipped back to Japan at the
earliest opportunity. The repatriation undoubtedly contradicts all known
international law. And the American Government did a fine propoganda job for their
widely advertised fundamental human rights! I am convinced that the American people
once acquainted with this affair would strongly denounce the action of their
government.
It may also interest you that several Nazis were allowed to remain in Japan
andcarry on their busines. Hereby the whole denazification appears to be nothing
but a farce and apparently the Allies bas quite a different objective in view when
they entered the war to free the German people and the world from nationlsocialism
and militarism!
Trusting that you will be in a position to rended us assistance in our plight
and help us to have thás apparent wrong righted. I am, sir,
Yours Respectfully,
HANS FRIEDEL
/s/ Hans Friedel
The Honorable Joseph MoCarthy
The United States Semate
Washingtom, D.C.

Mies1on's despatches no. 35 of January 19, 1949, and no. 101 of
February 16, 1949, concerning SCAP approval of the program permitting
entry of individuals into Japan for sixty-day visits to relatives who
894.111/7-649
are Japanese nationals or foreign nationals resident in Japan since
1
the beginning of World War II, and to forward for the information of
the Department, SCAP memorandum for the Japanese Government no.
AG 095 (22 May 48)GA of June 28, 1949 (SCAPIN 1971/3), which expands
the aforesaid program to permit entry to visit friends as well as
relatives. Other conditions for entry, such as sources of housing
and logistic support, and method of making application, remain
unchanged.
Local 1nformation indicates that approximately 1200 individuals
have been processed to enter Japan as visitors since the institution
of the program 1n February 1949. No significant difficulties have
been encountered in implementation of the program to date, according
to SCAP sections directly concerned.

PROCEDURES FOR IMPLEMENTATION OF
PROGIRM FOR ENTRY OF INDIVIDUALS
TO VISIT REL.TIVES AND FRIENDS
1. a. Prospective visitors to Japan will make application to a com-
mercial cirrier or travel agency for entry into Japan to visit relativer
friends giving the necessary information and making necessary guarantees
shown on the application forn.
b. The comm reiil cirrier will forwird the application to the
Jpn s dov rnr. nt for considerition.
2. a. Upon reccipt of the application, the Japanese Government will
contact the prospective host to determine:
(1) Thit the host des ires to have said rclatives or friends
visit hin.
(2)
That the host has adequate accomnodations available for
his visitors.
b. hen the Japanese Government has reccived assurances that the
conditions outlined in pir 2: above have been fulfilled, the Japanese Govern-
nent will notify the Suprere Comwninder for the allied Powers that it has no
objection to ntry into Japan of the visitor. Information will include the
nane, n tionality, method of travel, port of entry, proposed dates of the
visit, and the name, address nd nationality of the host.
c. The Suprune Comnand r for the allied Powers will notify the
Japanese Gov rnn nt of fin:1 approval for ontry Japan of the prospective
guests, at which time the Japinese Government will notify the commercial
carrier ind the Conninding Genural, Eighth United States .rmy, that clearance
for entry Japan has bocn granted. Notification to Comnanding General, Eighth
United States army, will include details as outlined in par b above.
d. It will be the responsibility of the commercial carrier to no-
tify the guest of the pproval of his application to enter Japan.
3. Upon receipt of notification of the approval of his application,
the visitor may pply for his pissport (and in the case of United States
citizens, a military permit) and arrange for transportation by a commercial
carrier to Japan. .ppropriate uthorities have been notified that the appro-
val of the applicition constitutes approval from the Supreme Commander for
the allied Powers for the issuance of a passport and a military permit for
entry into Japan in the case of merican citizens and is considered suffici-
ent clearance for the entry into Japan of other than american citizens.
4. Upon arrival at port of debarkation in Japan, the visitor will file
proof of his arrangenents for departure from Japan with the Japanese Govern-
ment representative. appropriate notation will be made in the visitor's
passport to indicate his tourist status.

IRANIAN EMBASSY
WASHINGTON, D. C.
No. 1494.
The Iranian Ambas ador presents his
compliments to His Excellency the secretary of
State and has the honour to state that Mr. Majdi

DATE: August 30, 1949.

Mr. O'Brien

894.111/8-1849
DS this date received Military Permit No. 2086 stating that
Mr. Phiphatana Vanij Vongse, a Thai national, is cleared to enter
Japan between September 1, 1949 and October 1, 1949 to stay there
sixty days for trade purposes.
Mr. O'Brien telephoned Mrs. Flickinger and requested her to refer
to the application Mr. Vongse submitted for this permission. It was
his understanding that this application would show that Mr. Vongse
is presently at Bangkok and should receive his documentation from the
Thai Foreign Office. Mrs. Flickinger checked Mr. Vongse's application
and confirmed Mr. O'Brien's understanding.
Mrs. Flickinger stated that it was satisfactory for the Department
to immediately prepare a diplomatic note to the Thai Embassy informing
it of Mr. Vongse's clearance and in response to Mr. O'Brien's suggestion
stated that Mr. Vongse's clearance period could be changed from the
above to September 10 to October 10, 1949. This would allow time for
his clearance to be communicated without using up some of the validity
period.
Mr. 0'Brien stated permit No. 2086 would be returned to the Pacific
Military Permit Branch and Mrs. Flickinger stated that upon its receipt
a memorandum confirming this conversation would be sent to the Depart-
ment for its files.

(Translation)
BRAZILIAN EMBASSY
N-243/7(42)(52)02
The Brazilian Embassy-proeents ite eompliments
to the Department of State and has the honor to inform as follows:
2.
On January 27, 1949, Mr. H. L. Sturgis, of the
Department's Division of Protective Services, transmitted by
telephone to r. O. A. Dias Carneiro, Second Secretary of the
Embassy, a communication that the Department of State had received
from the United States Embassy at Nanking, to the effeet that the
Brazilian Embassy in that city had informed the American Embassy
that the amount of money necessary to cover transportation ex-
penses of Mrs.Hilde Wiebke to Brazil would be made available to the
Department of State by this Embassy.
Mr. Sturgis added that said
communication informed also that Mrs. Wiebke's passport, which
had been issued by the Brazilian Consulate at Shanghai, would be
4
sent to Japan, in order to be visaed by the American occupation
authorities there, at the request of the Brazilian consular autho-
rities referred to above.
3.
On February 4, 1949, Mr. Sturgis called Mr. Dias
Carneiro again to inform him that the Department of State had re-
ceived a communication from the American occupation authorities, in
Tokyo, stating that the granting of an exit visa from Japan to
Mrs. Wiebke and her minor son was being delayed by the fact that
the necessary funds for her repatriation had not been put at the
disposal of the Department cf State by the Embassy.
4.
On the 25th of that same month, Mr. Sturgis com-

=== Page 416 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Emb.Brazi1/N•243/7(42)(52)02/1949.
communicated that the matter had been brought to the attontion
of the United States Embassy at Rio de Janeiro.
5.
All this information was transmitted to the Ministry
of Foreign Affairs in Rio de Janeiro, which replied that Mrs. Hilde
Wiebke and her minor son, both beng Brazilian citizens, were in
process of repatriation from Dairen, Manchuria, to Rio de Janoiro,
through Tokyo, at the expense of her relatives in Brasil, and that
these expenditures and the cost of transportation had been paid
to the agency of Pan American World Airways in Tokyo.
6.
The information in paragraph five above was communi-
cated by telephone to r. Sturgis by Mr. Dias Carneiro, on March
7, 1949.
7.
Recently, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs instruoted
this Embassy to request the Department's good offices with a view
towards ascertaining the possibility of obtaining transportation
for Mrs. Wiebke and her son who are still in Dairen, Manchuria, to
Tokyo, Japan, from where they will immediately proceed to Brazil,
since, as it was informed above, their transportation by air from
Tokyo to Rio de Janeiro has already been paid.
8.
The Brazilian Embessy would be very grateful to the
Department of State for the verification of the possibility of
transporting Mrs. iebke and her minor son from Dairen, Manchuria,
to Tokyo, Japan, and expresses its appreciative thanks in advance
for the kind attention of the Department to the matter.
Washington, August 24, 1949.

The Ministry of Foreign Relations has the
honor to bring to the attention of the Embassy of the

to the attention of the American authorities, the momo-
randum presented by the Fábrica Nacional de Tejidos de

"MR. MINISTER OF FOREIGN RELATIONS.—
With the attached documents and for the reasons explained
therein, we request diplomatic action toward authorisation
to leave the country indicated therein, in favor of the
persons mentioned therein.----/Otrosi.-Domicilio.7------
The memberS of FABRICA NACIONAL DE TEJIDOS DE PUNTO S.A.

established in this city, competent by law, respectfully
declare before you as follows: Our industrial firm,
composed of associates of Bolivian and Japanese nation-
ality, has its juridical personality and its Statutes
approved by Supreme Resolution No. 27220 of December 31,
1947.---For the better industrial management of our
factory, and on account of his particular technical
specialization in the manufacture of textiles, the
personal collaboration of Mr. TOMAS TAMIICHI KOMORI 1s
necessary to us; he is the legitmate brother of our
principal associate, Mr. Santiago T. Komori, and is
presently residing in Japan, actually in the small city
called Minomachi, Tokiwacho Street, Mugi-Gun Province,
Gifu-Ken Department of that country.----Mr. Tomas Tamiichi
Komori, engaged in commercial activities, resided in this
city of La Paz for a period of 27 years, or from January 30,
1917 to May 15, 1944, on which date he voluntarily
traveled to Japan, via the United of American and he has
not been able to return up to this time on account of
restrictions imposed as a result of the last World War.----
The aforementioned Mr. Komori, during his long stay in
Bolivia, has always observed the best conduct, as shown
by the

The act ion of the Department in the case of Sister
Angela NIn 1, an Italian national, has been noted.
The Department is inforimed that when Sister Ninett1
first called at tne Embassy to request its assistance in
this matter, she was referred to the Italian Embassy in
London. She later returned ana statei that she was informed
in that office tnat they could not assist her in obtaining
an entry permit for Japan. In the absence of information
to indicate that Italy maintains a diplomatic mission in
Japan, her application was accepted.

the advisability of proviaing diplomatic and consular
establishments with a current list of countries wnich
have diplomatic representatives in Japan as well as a
list of countries represented in Japan by Protecting
Powers, to assist them in handling this work.

TO MHOM IT MAY CONCERN:
This Will introduce Reverend Paul Gerhardt Möller of Liebenzell,
Germany. Dr. Moller is Professor of Theology of the Theological Seminary
of the Liebenzeller Mission, and 1s also the Direetor of that Mission. At
the present time he 1s makinp a tour of the Liebenzeller and other German
mission work in various parts of the world. This tour is being ponsored
by, and is under the generel direstion of the Intornational Missionary
Council. The funds therefore are being suppliod by the Church Missionary
Society of Britain.
H
Dr. Moller has a proper passport which indicates that he has
been permitted to visit various countries in Europe, and also has visas
for the United States and for entry into China. In connection with his
tour Dr. Mol er will visit Mioronesia with the permission and by the
courtesy of the nited States Navy.
he plan also to sec re permission from the proper authorities for
him to visit Japan and stil? hope that he will be able to get into China.
For the return journey to Germany, Dr. Möller hopes to embark
from Hongkong, probably via the Peninsular and Oriental Steamship Company
which will touch at Singapore. He will need permission to stop at Ceylon
for one or two weeks (between ships) and similarly at Port Said. He hopes
to disembark at Marseilles, and to proceed to Germany.
The International Missionary Council will greatly appreciate the
courtesy of the various Consulates and passport control offices conoerned
in granting Dr. Moller the requisite travel permissions.
The International Missionary Council unqualifiedly guarantees the
good faith and reliability of Dr. Möller, and that funds will be available
for the journey.

S00 894.111/6-2449
USPOLAD,
TOKYO.
A-175, 0ct. 14, 1949
On July 5, 1949, the Department sent the following airgram to
USPOLAD Berlin:
"On Jume 10, 1949, one Hans FRIEDEL, 33, Gartenstrasse,
Ludwigsburg, Wurttemberg, Germany, addressed a letter to
Senator Joseph McCarthy of Wisconsin, as follows:
He lived in Japan from 1933 to 1947, working as a baker
and as an instructor to Japanese bakers. In 1935 he married a
Japanese national in accordance with Japanese rites, but this
111/10-449
marriage not recognized by German consul at Yokohama, since it
conflicted with existing racial laws of Nazis. Friedel states
that he never was a member of the Nazi party or affiliated
organization; in fact he claims to have been arrested and
imprisoned in 1945 for his anti-Nazi activities. In August
1945, after an imprisonment of three months, he was set free
by US troops. Under orders issued by Headquarters Eighth
Army, Friedel, his wife, and minor child were forcibly
repatriated to Germany aboard American vessel Marine Jumper
in February 1947. Friedel feels that he and his family were
dealt with unjustly, since he, an anti-Nazi, was forced
to leave Japan while certain known Nazis were permitted to
remain in that country and carry on their businesses. He
requests Senator McCarthy to assist him in returning to
Japan.
Only record here on Friedel is mention of his name in
Deutsche Gemeinde, Tokyo-Yokohama, January 1938, which lists
him as non-member. Please ascertain background of case and
Friedel's eligibility for exit permit..."
HICOG Frankfurt advised the Department on October 4, 1949, that the
Combined Travel Board had already informed Friedel that there is no objection
to his being issued an exit permit, provided he obtain authorization to entor
Japan.
DISTRIBUTION
Please advise Department as to Friedel's eligibility to enter Japan.
DEGIRED
Acheson
(OFFICES ONLY)
Acbeson (ugm)
wk
DLIsBIsWTKamprad:wk 10/10/49

TO:
CERTAIN AMERICAN DIPLOMATIC OFFICERS.
SUBJECT:
Regulations governing application for permission to enter Japan.
REFERENCE: FSS 667, January 17, 1947.
1. SCAP Circular No. 8, August 30, 1946 (enclosure to Fss 667) providee
that: "Request for authorization to enter Japan will be made to the Surre
Commander for the Allied Powers by the representative in Japan of the country
whose national desires to enter Japan." For your information and guidance
the following countries presently maintain missions at Tokyo:
Australia
Denmark
Netherlands
Sweden
Belgium
France
Norway
Switzerland
Brazil
India
Philippines
United Kingdom
894.111/10-549
Canada
Italy
Portugal
Union of Seviet
China
Korea
Spain
Socialist Republics
2. SCAP has since amended Circular No. 8 to provide that applications
for permission to enter Japan of nationals of those governments which do not
maintain a diplomatic mission at Tokyo and whose interests in Japan are pro-
tected by third Powers shall be submitted to SCAP by such protecting Powers.
3. The following is a list of countries whose interests in Japan are
presently being protected by third Powers:
Country
Protecting Power

31

the formalities required of Soviet personnel were simi-
larly applicable to all Occupation personnel and accord-
ingly must be observed.
Until recently, clearance for Soviet entries and
departures into and from Japan had been governed by an
interim procedure established in l947 whioh permitted
the Soviet Member to prepare composite personal property
declarations in connection with the entries and departures
of all Soviet personnel (with the exception of General
3XRs
Derevyanko and one or two of nis senior officers who were
considered as having diplomatic status). In as muoh as
Headquarters has reoently become aware of certain Soviet
abuses of the privileges thus aceorded, however, it was
recently deoided that a strioter control over Soviet en
tries and departures would be inaugurated with a view 
preventing further abuses, this being done by ruling that
henceforth Soviet personnel would be subfeot to the same
regulations as those governing all Occupation and foralen
mission personnel other than acoredited diplomats.
Following Soviet objeotions to the applioation of
oustoms and immigration regulations in conneotion with
the entry of Soviet personnel on a Soviet plane

Dear General Derevyanko:
With reference to the misunderstanding that pre-
vailed among the crew of the Russian airoraft IL12 No. 3
on 3 September l949, during routine customs inspeotion at
Haneda Airport, it is desired to clarify the existing ous-
toms, immigration end quarantine procedures.
Prior temporary and interim agreements and instruo-
tions exempting Soviet personnel from routine inspection
have been superseded by the following oiroulars, whioh ap-
ply to all entrants in Japan, promulgated by General Head-
quarters, Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers:
Circular 9, 29 Maron 1948, subjeot,
"Foreign Quarantine Regulations for Japan".
as amended by Ciroular 26, 12 August 1948,
and Ciroular 17, 30 July 1949.
Ciroular 19, 23 June 1948, subjeot,
"Control of Entry and Exit of Individuals,
Aircraft and Surface Vessels into and from
Japan", as amended by Ciroular 27, 13 August
1948。
Ciroular 21, 26 June 1948, subjeot,
"Control of Entry and Exit of Property into
and from Japan".
Copies of the above-mentioned Ciroulars, whioh
set forth the customs, immigration and quarantine regula-
tions presently in efreot, which should be in your pos-
session, are inolosed for ready reference in case your
copies have been misplaced. It will be noted that these
regulations are equally applioable to all individuals
entering Japan, inoluding foreign mission personnel and
representatives of the Allied Powers accredited to the
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers. Eroeptions are
made only by speciric authorisation in individuel cases
involving diplomatio personnel or high officials who
would be acoorded oustoms courtesies under generelly ac
cepted international practice.

ALLIED COUNCIL FOR JAPAN
TOKYO
OFFICE OF THE MEMBER
FOR
THE UNION OF SOVIET SOCIALIST REPUBLICS
SUPREME COMMANDER FOR THE ALLIED POWERS
GENERAL OF THE ARMY DOUGLAS MBCARTHUR
Dear General:
A Soviet airplene with two diplomatic couriers with
diplomatic mail and several members of the staff of the
Soviet Section of the Allied Council for Japan were sched-
uled to depart from Haneda Airdrome for Vladivostok on 24
September 1949. When the above persons, accompanied by
members of the Soviet Section in charge of dispatohing
the plane, arrived at the eirdrome, representatives of
GHQ SCAP and American customs officials demanded to in-
spect the baggage of the r:embers of the Soviet Seotion of
the Allied Council for Japen. As is known, up to this
time no such inspection has been made, although repeated
attempts at such unlawful actions toward Soviet person-
nel in Japan have been made previously. After it be-
came clear that this matter could not be settled on the
spot, I ordered that the airplane be dispatohed with only
the diplomatic couriers and diplomatic mail aboard. How-
ever, representatives of GHQ and the customs officials
demanded to inspect also the personal baggage of the orew
of the airplene, whioh aotion is likewise without justifi-
cation and has not taken place in the past. As a result
of the above unlawful aotivities of Amerioan authorities,
the diplomatic couriers and diplomatio mail were not dis-
patched. It should also be pointed out that, upon arriv-
ing at the eirdrome, the Soviet officers and their col-
leagues were conducted by an especially augmented escort
of American military police, which had likewise never be-
fore been so numerous and in such bad taste.
I emphatically protest against the unlawful delaying
of the dispatoh of diplomatic couriers with diplomatio
mail, and also against eny attempts by Amerioan customs
officials to inspect the baggage of Soviet personnel and
the personal effeots of the orew of a Soviet plane.
In accordance with the Potsdam Declaration and the
decisions of the Moscow Conference of Three Ministers.
of Foreign Affairs, the Soviet Union, in the person of
the Member of the Allied Counoil for Japan from UBaR,
with

=== Page 558 === (confidence 0.99)
Enolosure No. 2 to Tokyo's
Despatoh No. 712 dated
Ootober 13, 1949.
-2-
with a starf of military and civilian advisors, parttoi-
pates in the maintenance of ocoupational control over
Japan, and therefore can not acknowledge the right of
American occupation authorities to institute suoh unlew-
ful activities in connection with the staff of the Mem-
ber of the Allied Counoil for Japan from USSR as the in-
spection of their baggage and personal effeots.
I insist upon the continuation of the existing pro-
cedure in conneotion with the baggage and personal effeots
of Soviet personnel; I request that you issue orders to
this effect to the customs authorities.
At the same time, I insist that the disoriminatory
procedure of escorting Soviet officers and their colleagues
at the airdrome by American military police be eliminated.
Respectfully yours,
MEMBER, ALLIED COUNCIL FOR
JAPAN FROM THE U.S.S.R.
General-Leitenant

GENERAL HEADQUARTERS
SUPRENE COMMANDER FOR THE ALLTED POWERS
25 September 1949
Lieutenant General Tuzme N. Derevyanko
The Soviet Member
Allied Counc il for Japan
Dear General Derevyanko:
Receipt is acknowledged of your letter of 24 Septem-
ber l94? to the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
in whioh you take the position that members of the staff
of the Soviet Section of the Allied Council for Japan
should not be subiect to the routine oustoms inspeotion
upon arrival in or departure from Japan.
The customs inspections were established in conform
ance with regulations promulgated by General Headquarter
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers and apply, with-
out distinction, to all individuals entering or leaving
Japan; regulations such as are 1mposed by every nation
in the world. Copies of these regulations were trans-
mitted to you in the initial distribution and again as
enclosures to our letter of 19 September 1949.
Since your letter of 24 September questions the
authority or the Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers
to apply routine customs inspections to the staff of the
Soviet Member of the Allied Counoil for Japan, it is ap-
propriate here to dispel this misapprehension on your
part. Paragraph 5 of the terms of reference for the
Allied Council for Japan, as agreed upon at the confer-
ence of Foreign Ministers at Moscow, states inpart:
"The Supreme Commander shall issue all orders
for the implementation of the Terms of Surrender,
the ocoupation and control of Japan, and direotives
supplementary thereto. In all cases aotion will
carried out under and through the Bupreme Commandex
who is the sole exeoutive authority for the Allied
Powers in Japan."

=== Page 560 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 3 to Tokyo's
Despatoh No. 712 dated
Ootober 13, 1949.
-2-
Thus, it should be clear not only that the authority
to issue the orders in question resides in the Supreme
Commander, but also that this authority is not ahared by
any other Allied agenoy or individual in Japan.
It is also proper to refer to an inoidental issue
respeoting the clearance for departure of a member of the
Soviet group on 24 September 1949. The Soviet Member,
Allied Counoil for Japan, was informed by momorandum
dated 19 September 1949 that olearance. for Mr. S. Y.
Fomichev would be considered only when he had submitted
complete technical reports to General Headquarters,
Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers, as required by
the policy directive of the Far Eastern Commission, FEC
280/9 "Access to Japanese Scientifio and Technical In-
formation in Japan". Notwithstanding the provisions of
this directive that "Technical and soientific information
obtained *** be promptly and fully disclosed to SCAp ***n
and the notification in our memorandum that this had not
been complied with, it appears that the departure of Mr.
Fonichev was contemplated on 24 September without proper
clearance. Since the provisions of this FEC direotive
have been transmitted to you and should, by now, be well
understood, the failure to comply is quite incomprehensi-
ble。
By the foregoing, the purpose and validity of the
routine inspections and clearance requirements have been
clarified, and it is emphasized that they must be observed
with the proper degree of complience by the staff of the
Soviet Member of the Allied Council for Japan in the same
manner as is now being done by all other individuals, in-
cluding all Amerioan personnel from the Supreme Commander
down through all who are subordinate to him, entering and
leaving Japan, with the usual exceptions accorded individ-
uals with bona fide diplomatic status.
FOR THE SUPREME COMMANDER:
EDWARD M. ALMOND
Major General, General Staff Corps
Chief of Staff

Shipping Company, Inc. to supervise the construction of an 8,500 tons cargo
1949
liner contracted for with Yokohama Ship Yard & Engine Works, Mitsubishi

C
October, November 1950.

construction.

Division of Protective Services,
Dear Mr. Madill:
At the time of my visit at your office last September 1 on behalf
of my mother, Mrs. Anna Slenkina, you gave me a faint ray of hope
in saying that it might be possible to have her come to the United
States on a boat scheduled to leave the Far East the latter part
894
of September.
In the meantime two letters were received from my aunt, Mrs. V.N.
Shepitkovsky (sister of Mrs. Anna Slenkina). Mrs. Shepitkovsky
resides in Harbin, Manchuria, China, and her two letters came via
two of her friends, one of whom was traveling to Poland and the
other to Tientsin.
10-2749
Mrs. Shepitkovsky wrote that my mother, Mrs. Slenkina, had her visa
to the United States and also an exit visa fron U.S.S.R. to leave
Dairen, and that she also had money and & passage arranged for her
via air from Japan to the United States. From the dates of her
letters we could tell that Mrs. Slenkina must have been all set to
503/L0/0）
leave Dairen sometime around the middle of June. Still at this
time we have no further news.
C
Is there some possible way that we could establish the reason for S
the delay and whether there is anything further that we can possibly
R
do to expedite things a bit.
In regard to Mrs. Shepitkovsky, her daughter Mrs. L.O. Larson of
Minneapolis, Minn. is acting on her behalf and is equally anxious
to be of some further help in having her mother come to this country.
It seems that Mrs. Shepitkovsky was granted a "preference status",
sometime ago, and that this status is no longer in effect since Mrs.
Shepitkovsky was not able to get to a Consulate of U.S.A. to make
her application for a visa. If there is anything that I can possibly
do on behalf of Mrs. Shepitkovsky to help Mrs. Larson have her
preference status reinstated, I would be only too happy to do so.
Would appreciate greatly if you could possibly communicate with the
CA. state. ffigat
217690
Dairen is closed

DC
compliments to His Excellency the Secretary of State and
has the honor to reguest that authorization be sought
with the appropriate authorities of the United States
ncl
Government for seüor William Reuben to enter Japan.

of Economy of Costa Rica to visit Japan, to discuss person-
ally with the occupation authorities, the local authorities
and the local trade organizations, as well as with private
business the possibility of developing trade relations
m
between Costa Rica and Japan.

Memorandum of Conversation
DATE: November 14,1
SUBJECT:
Permission to enter Japan for Mr. and Mrs. Pavel Tsemahovich
Kraslavsky and minor child.
PARTICIPANTS:
Mr. Walter Best, President, Pacific Mercantile Corporation,
309 Seventh Avenue, New York 1, New York,
telephone Algonquin 5-3835.
Mrs. Mellor - DS
COPIES TO:
On November 8, 1949 Mr. Best called Mrs. Mellor and informed her
that an application had that day been presented to the regional office
of the Department of Commerce at New York for permission for Mr. Kraslavsky
CS/J
to enter Japan as the representative of the Pacific Mercantile Corporation.
Mr. Best also said that Mr. Kraslavsky desired his wife and minor child
to accompany him to Japan.
Mrs. Mellor discussed the matter of Mrs. Kraslavsky and her child
accompanying Mr. Kraslavsky to Japanwiththe Pacific Military Permit Branch.PMp
told her that Lirs. Kraslavsky should send an application to the Department
giving her place and date of birth and her nationality status. Not until
her nationality is known could the PuPB state whether it could process her
application.
On Monday, November 14 Mrs. Mellor called Mr. Best and gave him the
foregoing information. Mr. and Mrs. Kraslavsky are now in Cuba and
Mr. Best will obtain the necessary information and submit it to the
Department. He stated that Mrs. Kraslavsky is stateless. He was also
told that it is important for Mr. and Mrs. Kraslavsky to state what kind
of documents they are traveling on, their number, issuing authority and
validity dates, and that they must have a permit to enter some other
country after the expiration of their stay in Japan. Mr. Best said he
would obtain the required information
